Reference is made to my memorandum of September 23, Tele. Room
1957,
on the captioned matter which reflected an Intelli-
Holloman _
gence Advisory Committee (IAG) evaluation of an Air Defense
Gandy T
Command (ADC) report that radar stations at Kontauk Point,
Long Island,
New Jork, and Benton, Pennsylvania, detected
unidentified object proceeding in a westward direction with
an altitude of 50,000 feet and a speed of 2, 000 knota
(approzimately 2,300 miles per hour) on september 20, 195%.
The IAC intelligence evaluation of this report reflected
that "It is highly improbable that a Soviet operation is
responsible for the
september 20, 1957, "nidentified flying object reports of di
-..
Today, at an Executive Session of the IAC,
General Willard Lewis, Director, Air Force Intelligence,
advised that although the ADO has not completed its investi
gation of the evidence or this matter, there are continuing
indications that the object detected vas an atmospheric
phenomenon.
The radar pickups now reflect speed variations
in the object's course, ranging from 1,500 miles per hour to
4,500 miles per hour.
This latter speed is improbable
according to U.s. scientific theory for any type flying
objeot which this could conceivably be. General Lewis added
the present sun spots are associated with the peculiar
radar activity throughout the globe and that this could have
some cause for the captioned report.
ACTION:
Liaison will report further information on this
matter as quickly as
is developed.

UNIDENTIFIED FLYING OBJECT REPORTED
ON SEPTEIBER 20, 1957; INTELLIGENCE

The following information was obtained by Liaison
Superoisor Y.W. Kuhrtz at the Intelligence Aduisory Committee - Watch
Committee special meeting or September 21,
1957, relative to the
captioned matter.
The initial report in this matter was given by
O.5. Air Defense Command to the White House on September 20, 195%,
reflecting that an unidentified flying object was picked up by radar
station at Hontauk Point, long Island, New York, at approtimately
LENA SE
4 p.m., proceeding in a westward direction with an altitude of
50,000 feet and a speed of 2,000 knots (approrimately 2300 miles per
nour). The radar station at Montaut Point maintained an image for
approcinately one minute, and thereafter it was picked up by a radar
coincided with the wontouk Point pickup and vas tracked by Benton for
approtimately 9 minutes. Thereafter, jamming was reported by several
radar stations westward as far as Chicago.
The iratch Committee at its meeting on September 21 was told
by the Air Defense Command that the original reports had beer down-
graded, since there was an Il-minute break in the tracking by the
various radad stations, and further that weather :stations; in the
area were of the type which have in the past produced false radar
"pips" and electronio information. Also, there were a number of
Strategio dir Command planes in the Chicago area on a training flight.
The Watch Committee was told that the dir Defense Command has not
completed its investigation of this incident; however, in any event
it was reported that the phenomena reported west of Buffalo, New York,
were not related to the object as reported by Montauk Point and
Benton, Pennsyluaria.
The watch Committee concluded that, "It is highly improbable
that a soviet operation is responsible for the unidentified flying
object reports of september, 1957."
The watch Committee in consideting
this matter also concluded that there is no intelligence on soviet
activities which can be related to a missile lounching of this
type

ADVISOPY COLMITTEE - VATCH COMMITTEE
over the U.S. It is unlikely that a souiet aircraft could conduct
a mission at this
speed and altitude and return to soviet territory.
The Soviets are given credit for the capability of a 'submarine
launched cruise-type missile
of low supersonio performance" and o
range of about 500 nautical miles; however, there is no evidence
of the eristence of such a missile by the soviets.
The Watch Committee also eramined possible Soviet motives
Sor launching o one way missile on an operation over the U.S., and
it was concluded that there would be little motivation at this time
except possibly s psychological or retaliatory motive.
The Hatch
Committee ruled out one way reconnaissance operations
on the likelihood
that the results would be of small value and the risk of compromise
would be very great. (This possible retaliatory motive by the soviets
could be in
response tator flighte to a U.s.
"Project Aquatone"
flight over the USSR which was detected on September 9,
1956, by
soviet planes and mentioned in my memorandum of September 19, 1957.)
